But once they figured out the buttress, they could open up the walls with all that magnificent stained glass. I actually took my wife to Chartres on our honeymoon. We took this terrible little train from Paris, and it was raining the entire way. The seats were covered in this awful scratchy green wool, and the man sitting across from us kept eating these incredibly pungent garlic sausages... \n\nBut, um... I'm sorry, I seem to be drifting again. What was it you were asking? I know we just talked about enjoying things, but was there something else?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray tone underlying everything?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a while now. It really started after my hiking injury. That was a little over three years ago... maybe three years and three months? Yes, because it was right around the time my daughter bought her first house. She got this lovely little craftsman bungalow in Portland. Needs a lot of work, though. The foundation is settling on the west side, which I told her she needs to get looked at immediately. You don't want to mess around with differential settlement, it'll crack the drywall right up to the ceiling. And her husband, bless his heart, he thinks he can fix everything with a tube of caulk and some two-by-fours. I tried to explain to him about load-bearing walls when they wanted to open up the kitchen, but he just... \n\nAnyway, yes, it's been about three years and three months since the injury. That's when this whole gray feeling really settled in.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Activities... well, I keep trying to come up with something for you. I suppose I still try to take walks through the botanical gardens. We've had a membership there for years. The azalea bloom in the spring used to be spectacular. They have this one variety, a sort of deep magenta—'Formosa', I believe it's called. Or maybe 'George Lindley Taber'. No, George is a lighter pink, almost white with a speckled throat. \n\nAnyway, we had a neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, who tried to grow those exact azaleas in her front yard, but the soil in our neighborhood is far too alkaline. I told her, I said, 'Margaret, you need to amend that soil with pine bark and elemental sulfur,' but she insisted on using this cheap, bagged topsoil she bought at the grocery store. You can't just plant acid-loving shrubs in any old dirt and expect a miracle. She ended up digging them up and replacing them with these dreadful plastic flamingos. Just awful, tacky things. They faded to a sort of sickly salmon color after just one summer in the sun... \n\nBut, ah... no. No, to answer your question, I really can't think of anything. Even going to the gardens now, it just feels like a chore. Just a lot of walking for no real reason.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: Small pleasures... well, I suppose I used to look forward to my morning coffee. We had this old French press, one of the glass ones, not those insulated stainless steel monstrosities they sell nowadays. But my wife accidentally cracked it against the edge of the granite countertop last month. I always told her those granite edges were too sharp, we should have gone with a bullnose edge or maybe an ogee, but she liked the clean lines of the square edge. Of course, the contractor we hired to install it—Frank, I think his name was—he did a terrible job sealing the seam near the sink. I had to get under there myself with a flashlight and a tube of silicone. You know, if water gets down into those seams, it can ruin the cabinetry underneath. The particle board just swells up like a sponge... \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, looking forward to things. No... no, not really. Even the coffee doesn't taste like much anymore, no matter how we make it. I just don't have anything I'm really anticipating.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... I'm not really sure. I haven't really noticed that, exactly. I mean, I still care about keeping my things in good order. Like my tools, for instance. I have this beautiful set of Marples woodworking chisels—the original ones, made in Sheffield, England, not the cheap ones they mass-produce overseas now. The high-carbon steel they used back then holds an edge beautifully, provided you actually know how to maintain them. \n\nOf course, you have to be meticulous. I use a Japanese water stone, an 8000 grit, to get a proper mirror finish on the bevel. You wouldn't believe it, but a few years ago, my brother-in-law borrowed my half-inch chisel to pry open a rusted can of deck stain. A precision instrument, treated like a common flathead screwdriver! It chipped the blade right in the center. I had to spend an entire afternoon on a coarse diamond plate just to grind past the damage before I could even start honing it again. I told him, I said, 'Richard, if you need a pry bar, go to the hardware store, don't ruin a fifty-dollar chisel.' He just laughed it off. People just have absolutely no respect for proper craftsmanship these days, they treat everything like it's disposable... \n\nBut, ah... what was the question? Have I lost interest? I don't really know. I don't think I've noticed a change like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Well, I suppose I haven't really noticed any change. My clothes still fit about the same. I've always bought my trousers from this one specific tailor downtown, a lovely old fellow named Mr. Aris. He's Greek, moved here in the seventies, I think. He uses this fantastic worsted wool for his winter trousers, the kind that drapes perfectly and holds a crease even if you've been sitting on a train for three hours. \n\nYou know, the last time I went in there, he told me his landlord was trying to raise the rent by nearly forty percent. Forty percent! Can you imagine? It's absolute robbery. These commercial real estate conglomerates come in, buy up the historic buildings, and just squeeze out all the independent craftsmen. They did the same thing to the cobbler two streets over. Now it's just another one of those trendy juice bars where they charge you nine dollars for a cup of blended kale and celery. I tried one once, just out of curiosity, and it tasted exactly like lawn clippings. I told the girl behind the counter, I said, 'I could get the same experience by opening my mouth while mowing the lawn,' but she didn't find it very amusing. No sense of humor in the younger generation... \n\nBut, uh, weight. No, I don't think I've lost any weight. Not that I've noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find give you some sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: Activities... pleasure. Well, I suppose I used to spend a good deal of time in my study reading. I have quite an extensive collection of historical biographies, mostly nineteenth-century European history. The physical books themselves used to be a real joy to handle. They used actual smyth sewing for the bindings back then, not this perfect-binding nonsense they use now where they just slap some cheap glue on the spine. If you open a modern hardback too wide, you can actually hear the adhesive crack, and the pages start falling out like autumn leaves. \n\nI bought a new biography of Gladstone just last month, paid thirty-five dollars for it, and by chapter four, the entire first section had completely detached from the cover. I sat down and wrote a letter to the publisher about their abysmal quality control. Do you know what they did? They sent back an automated email. Not even a physical letter on proper stationery, just an email from a no-reply address. It is just perfectly indicative of the general decline in standards across the board. Nobody takes any pride in their work anymore, it is all just about cutting corners...\n\nBut, um... what were you asking? Activities that give me pleasure. Honestly, no. I don't really get any enjoyment out of the reading these days, or anything else I try to do. I just sort of sit there and pass the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: Thoughts of not wanting to live? Oh, no. No, I haven't had any thoughts like that. I mean, I suppose I just keep going, day by day. It reminds me of this old grandfather clock I inherited from my great-uncle Arthur. It's a beautiful piece, mahogany case, brass dial, made sometime in the late 1800s. \n\nIt used to keep perfect time, absolute precision. But a few years ago, the mechanism started catching. I called around to find a horologist—a proper one, mind you, not some fellow who just changes watch batteries at the mall kiosk. Do you know how hard it is to find someone who actually understands a deadbeat escapement these days? I finally found a chap three towns over. He had the nerve to tell me he couldn't get the parts and suggested I just replace the entire movement with a quartz mechanism. A battery-operated quartz mechanism in a nineteenth-century antique! I nearly hung up the phone on him. It's an absolute travesty how we just discard history because it's slightly inconvenient to maintain. I ended up having to source a replacement escape wheel from a specialist in Germany and install it myself. Took me three weeks to get the tension right on the pendulum...\n\nBut, ah, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Thoughts of wishing I were dead? No, no, nothing like that. I just... keep ticking along, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Interfering with my ability to function? Well, I suppose I still manage the basics around the house. The laundry gets done, the bills get paid. Though speaking of bills, the water company just switched to this entirely paperless billing system. They didn't even ask, just sent an email saying, 'Congratulations, you're saving the planet!' and stopped mailing the statements. \n\nNow you have to log into this dreadful online portal that demands a password with a capital letter, a number, a special character, and heaven knows what else. I wrote the password down in a little leather-bound notebook I keep in my desk—a lovely piece of Italian leather, real craftsmanship, not that faux-polyurethane nonsense they sell at office supply stores now. But of course, the website updates every three months and forces you to change the password anyway. I spent forty-five minutes on the phone with a customer service representative last Tuesday who sounded like he was standing in a wind tunnel, trying to explain that I just wanted a piece of paper mailed to my house so I could write a check. A physical check! But apparently, that makes me some sort of dinosaur...\n\nWhat was the question? Ah, functioning. Yes, well, I still manage the house. But I have certainly stepped back from things. I used to do a bit of mentoring for the young graduates in my old field, and I'd go to the community board meetings, the music gatherings... I even had some travel plans mapped out for the autumn. But I've just let all of that go. My weeks are... well, they're just much smaller than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago? I... I don't really think so. It's just been this sort of steady, gray baseline for a long time now. It reminds me of the weather we had last November, actually. Just day after day of this unrelenting, flat overcast sky. \n\nI remember I was trying to get the gutters cleaned out before the first freeze. I hired this young fellow from down the street, the Miller boy. Nice enough lad, but completely lacking in any sort of situational awareness. He brought over this aluminum ladder—flimsy thing, looked like it would buckle if a strong breeze hit it—and proceeded to lean it directly against the aluminum siding of the house. No stand-off arms, no padding, nothing. Just metal grinding against metal. I had to go out there and explain to him that he was going to scratch the paint right down to the bare siding. Do you know what he said? He asked if I had an app he could download to watch a tutorial on how to use a ladder properly. An app! To use a ladder! I ended up having to go into the garage and fetch my old wooden extension ladder. Solid oak, brass fittings, heavy as a dead horse, but it won't budge an inch once you set the feet. I bought it at a hardware store in 1982, back when hardware stores actually smelled like sawdust and motor oil, instead of just being giant warehouses selling plastic patio furniture and scented candles...\n\nBut, um... worse than a year ago? No, I don't think worse. Just... the same. It's just been the same for a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any things that you look forward to or that bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Things I look forward to? Joy? Well, I... I suppose I don't really have much of an answer for that anymore. It's actually why I finally made the appointment to come in. I've just started to feel, in this very quiet but incredibly stubborn sort of way, that the best version of my life is entirely behind me. And no amount of planning or discipline or... or forcing myself to keep to a schedule is going to get me back to feeling like myself. \n\nIt's a bit like trying to restore that old leather armchair in my study. It’s a Chesterfield, original tufting, probably from the 1940s. I bought it at an estate sale years ago. The leather had started to dry out, you see, getting those microscopic cracks that eventually turn into full tears if you don't address them. I spent weeks researching the proper conditioning oils. You can't just slap any commercial leather balm on a piece like that; most of them are full of silicones that just sit on the surface and eventually suffocate the hide. I ended up ordering this specialized neatsfoot oil blend from a tannery in upstate New York. I spent three consecutive weekends carefully massaging it into the leather, letting it absorb, buffing it out. And it looked better, it really did. But the truth is, the structure underneath—the horsehair stuffing, the coil springs—it's all just tired. You can condition the surface all you want, but when you sit in it, you can feel that it just doesn't have the support it used to. It's never going to be the chair it was when it was built. \n\nAnd I suppose... well, what was the question? Ah, joy. Yes. I just... I don't think there's anything I'm really looking forward to. It just feels like the good parts are already finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are no longer able to experience pleasure from activities that you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Pleasure from things I used to enjoy? I suppose... well, I haven't really noticed it entirely disappearing, but it's just not the same. I used to get a great deal of satisfaction out of maintaining my garden, particularly the rose bushes. I have these lovely David Austin varieties, you know, the English roses with the incredible fragrance and all those densely packed petals. \n\nThey require an enormous amount of attention, of course. You have to prune them just right in the late winter, cutting at a 45-degree angle right above an outward-facing bud. And the black spot! It's a constant battle. I used to mix my own organic fungicide using baking soda and horticultural oil. I bought this beautiful copper sprayer from a catalog—solid copper, mind you, with a brass nozzle. It was a replica of a French vineyard sprayer from the 19th century. The weight of it alone was impressive, none of that cheap plastic that splits down the seam after one season in the sun. I remember spending an entire afternoon just polishing the copper until it gleamed like a new penny. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, came over and asked if I was preparing for a museum exhibit. She means well, but she has absolutely no appreciation for well-made tools. She hires a lawn service that comes tearing through her yard with those massive zero-turn mowers, just scalping the grass and throwing clippings all over the sidewalk...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, pleasure from activities. Yes, well, I don't know if I'd say I'm no longer able to experience it at all, but... I just don't really have the drive to do those things lately. I haven't even taken the copper sprayer out of the shed this year.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I can actually trace it back pretty clearly. It started right around the time I had that hiking injury. That was... gosh, a little over three years ago. About three years and three months, to be precise. \n\nI was up in the White Mountains, trying to do a section of the Appalachian Trail. I used to go up there every autumn. The foliage, you know, it's just spectacular if you catch it on the right weekend. Anyway, I had invested in this new pair of hiking boots. Italian leather, Vibram soles, the whole nine yards. I spent weeks breaking them in around the neighborhood. Walked miles on the pavement just to get the leather supple. But the thing they don't tell you about those high-end European boots is that the ankle support can be incredibly rigid until they're truly broken in on uneven terrain. \n\nSo there I was, coming down this rocky scramble near Mount Lafayette, and I placed my foot on what looked like a solid piece of granite. Turned out to be loose scree. My foot slipped, the rigid boot caught, and I went down hard. Twisted my knee terribly. I had to hobble three miles back to the trailhead using a walking stick I found on the ground. A nice piece of hickory, actually, stripped of its bark and smoothed down by the weather. I brought it home with me, thinking I'd sand it down and finish it with some linseed oil, maybe put a leather strap on the handle. I have this whole jar of brass tacks in the basement that would have looked wonderful studded around the grip. They're square-cut tacks, the kind they used to use for upholstery in the 19th century. You just can't find them at regular hardware stores anymore, you have to order them from specialty restoration catalogs...\n\nBut, yes, the injury. That was three years and three months ago. And I suppose that's when this whole... this whole flat, gray feeling really settled in. I just haven't been able to shake it since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

She hires a lawn service that comes tearing through her yard with those massive zero-turn mowers, just scalping the grass and throwing clippings all over the sidewalk...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, pleasure from activities. Yes, well, I don't know if I'd say I'm no longer able to experience it at all, but... I just don't really have the drive to do those things lately. I haven't even taken the copper sprayer out of the shed this year.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I can actually trace it back pretty clearly. It started right around the time I had that hiking injury. That was... gosh, a little over three years ago. About three years and three months, to be precise. \n\nI was up in the White Mountains, trying to do a section of the Appalachian Trail. I used to go up there every autumn. The foliage, you know, it's just spectacular if you catch it on the right weekend. Anyway, I had invested in this new pair of hiking boots. Italian leather, Vibram soles, the whole nine yards. I spent weeks breaking them in around the neighborhood. Walked miles on the pavement just to get the leather supple. But the thing they don't tell you about those high-end European boots is that the ankle support can be incredibly rigid until they're truly broken in on uneven terrain. \n\nSo there I was, coming down this rocky scramble near Mount Lafayette, and I placed my foot on what looked like a solid piece of granite. Turned out to be loose scree. My foot slipped, the rigid boot caught, and I went down hard. Twisted my knee terribly. I had to hobble three miles back to the trailhead using a walking stick I found on the ground. A nice piece of hickory, actually, stripped of its bark and smoothed down by the weather. I brought it home with me, thinking I'd sand it down and finish it with some linseed oil, maybe put a leather strap on the handle. I have this whole jar of brass tacks in the basement that would have looked wonderful studded around the grip. They're square-cut tacks, the kind they used to use for upholstery in the 19th century. You just can't find them at regular hardware stores anymore, you have to order them from specialty restoration catalogs...\n\nBut, yes, the injury. That was three years and three months ago. And I suppose that's when this whole... this whole flat, gray feeling really settled in. I just haven't been able to shake it since then.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like you're a burden to others or that life is not worth living?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A burden? Or... life not being worth it? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't think I'm a burden to anyone. I keep to myself mostly, and I certainly don't go around feeling like life isn't worth living. It's just... dull. 

But speaking of burdens, you wouldn't believe the property taxes they're trying to levy on us this year. The city council sent out these reassessment notices last month. Printed on this terribly cheap, glossy paper—the kind that smudges the ink if your thumb is even slightly damp. I actually wrote a letter to the mayor's office about it. I used my fountain pen, of course. It's a Montblanc Meisterstück, a gift to myself when I finished my master's degree decades ago. The nib is 14k gold, medium point. It writes like absolute butter if you use the right ink. I'm quite partial to this deep blue-black shade from a small company in Japan. They make it using traditional methods, grinding the pigments by hand. You have to be careful, though, because if you leave it in the pen too long without using it, it can clog the feed. I have a whole ultrasonic cleaning routine I do every three months just to keep it flowing perfectly. I use distilled water and just a single drop of this specialized surfactant... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right. Being a burden. No, no, I don't feel like a burden. I manage my own affairs just fine.

I suppose I came in because... lately I’ve started to feel, in this very quiet but just incredibly stubborn way, that the best version of my life is completely behind me. \n\nIt's funny, I was actually just looking through some old boxes in the attic the other day when it really hit me. I found all my old research notes from graduate school—I spent years studying architectural history, you know. I even found this old drafting compass my father gave me when I got my degree. It needs a new hinge screw, actually. I took it to a hardware store on 4th street, but the young kid working there didn't even know what it was, let alone have the parts for it. It’s a shame, really, they just don't make tools to last anymore. Everything is digital now. My grandson designs whole buildings on a tablet while sitting on the couch. \n\nBut, um... I'm sorry, what was I saying? Right, the clinic. I just... I used to be so structured, but now I feel like no amount of planning or discipline is going to get me back to feeling like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Well, sad... I suppose so. Most days I’m not in tears or anything dramatic like that. It’s really more just... there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything. Even when I’m out in my studio. I actually converted our old detached garage into a workspace a few years ago. Getting the permits for that was an absolute nightmare, let me tell you. The city zoning board wanted me to submit three different sets of elevations just to add a simple skylight. I told the clerk, I spent decades studying historical structures, I think I know how to properly frame a roof window. But they make you jump through all these bureaucratic hoops anyway. It took six months just to get the approval to pour a leveled floor. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. The mood. Yes, even when I'm out there in the studio finally, or just sitting across from my wife at dinner... she made this wonderful roast chicken last night, actually, though the grocery store was completely out of the fresh rosemary she usually buys... but yes, even then, that gray feeling is just always sitting there with us.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things... no, I suppose not. Not the way I used to. Like my architectural history books. I used to spend hours just leafing through the plates on Gothic cathedrals. I have this beautiful first edition on French masonry techniques—found it at a little used bookstore in Boston back in, oh, it must have been '82. The owner there, this fellow named Arthur, he always kept the best volumes in the back room for me. He had this old golden retriever that would sleep right under the cash register. You had to step over him to pay. I wonder whatever happened to that shop... they probably tore it down for condos. Everything in that neighborhood is condos now. \n\nBut, um... right, enjoying things. No, even when I sit down with my books now, it just feels like... like I'm going through the motions. I turn the pages, but the spark is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I think I just said that, didn't I? About the books? It's just... no, the pleasure isn't really there. It's like I was saying about the Gothic cathedrals. You know, it's fascinating how they managed to distribute the weight using flying buttresses. Before that, the walls had to be incredibly thick, which meant the windows had to be small, so the interiors were always so dark. But once they figured out the buttress, they could open up the walls with all that magnificent stained glass. I actually took my wife to Chartres on our honeymoon. We took this terrible little train from Paris, and it was raining the entire way. The seats were covered in this awful scratchy green wool, and the man sitting across from us kept eating these incredibly pungent garlic sausages... \n\nBut, um... I'm sorry, I seem to be drifting again. What was it you were asking? I know we just talked about enjoying things, but was there something else?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray tone underlying everything?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a while now. It really started after my hiking injury. That was a little over three years ago... maybe three years and three months? Yes, because it was right around the time my daughter bought her first house. She got this lovely little craftsman bungalow in Portland. Needs a lot of work, though. The foundation is settling on the west side, which I told her she needs to get looked at immediately. You don't want to mess around with differential settlement, it'll crack the drywall right up to the ceiling. And her husband, bless his heart, he thinks he can fix everything with a tube of caulk and some two-by-fours. I tried to explain to him about load-bearing walls when they wanted to open up the kitchen, but he just... \n\nAnyway, yes, it's been about three years and three months since the injury. That's when this whole gray feeling really settled in.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Activities... well, I keep trying to come up with something for you. I suppose I still try to take walks through the botanical gardens. We've had a membership there for years. The azalea bloom in the spring used to be spectacular. They have this one variety, a sort of deep magenta—'Formosa', I believe it's called. Or maybe 'George Lindley Taber'. No, George is a lighter pink, almost white with a speckled throat. \n\nAnyway, we had a neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, who tried to grow those exact azaleas in her front yard, but the soil in our neighborhood is far too alkaline. I told her, I said, 'Margaret, you need to amend that soil with pine bark and elemental sulfur,' but she insisted on using this cheap, bagged topsoil she bought at the grocery store. You can't just plant acid-loving shrubs in any old dirt and expect a miracle. She ended up digging them up and replacing them with these dreadful plastic flamingos. Just awful, tacky things. They faded to a sort of sickly salmon color after just one summer in the sun... \n\nBut, ah... no. No, to answer your question, I really can't think of anything. Even going to the gardens now, it just feels like a chore. Just a lot of walking for no real reason.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: Small pleasures... well, I suppose I used to look forward to my morning coffee. We had this old French press, one of the glass ones, not those insulated stainless steel monstrosities they sell nowadays. But my wife accidentally cracked it against the edge of the granite countertop last month. I always told her those granite edges were too sharp, we should have gone with a bullnose edge or maybe an ogee, but she liked the clean lines of the square edge. Of course, the contractor we hired to install it—Frank, I think his name was—he did a terrible job sealing the seam near the sink. I had to get under there myself with a flashlight and a tube of silicone. You know, if water gets down into those seams, it can ruin the cabinetry underneath. The particle board just swells up like a sponge... \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, looking forward to things. No... no, not really. Even the coffee doesn't taste like much anymore, no matter how we make it. I just don't have anything I'm really anticipating.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... I'm not really sure. I haven't really noticed that, exactly. I mean, I still care about keeping my things in good order. Like my tools, for instance. I have this beautiful set of Marples woodworking chisels—the original ones, made in Sheffield, England, not the cheap ones they mass-produce overseas now. The high-carbon steel they used back then holds an edge beautifully, provided you actually know how to maintain them. \n\nOf course, you have to be meticulous. I use a Japanese water stone, an 8000 grit, to get a proper mirror finish on the bevel. You wouldn't believe it, but a few years ago, my brother-in-law borrowed my half-inch chisel to pry open a rusted can of deck stain. A precision instrument, treated like a common flathead screwdriver! It chipped the blade right in the center. I had to spend an entire afternoon on a coarse diamond plate just to grind past the damage before I could even start honing it again. I told him, I said, 'Richard, if you need a pry bar, go to the hardware store, don't ruin a fifty-dollar chisel.' He just laughed it off. People just have absolutely no respect for proper craftsmanship these days, they treat everything like it's disposable... \n\nBut, ah... what was the question? Have I lost interest? I don't really know. I don't think I've noticed a change like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Well, I suppose I haven't really noticed any change. My clothes still fit about the same. I've always bought my trousers from this one specific tailor downtown, a lovely old fellow named Mr. Aris. He's Greek, moved here in the seventies, I think. He uses this fantastic worsted wool for his winter trousers, the kind that drapes perfectly and holds a crease even if you've been sitting on a train for three hours. \n\nYou know, the last time I went in there, he told me his landlord was trying to raise the rent by nearly forty percent. Forty percent! Can you imagine? It's absolute robbery. These commercial real estate conglomerates come in, buy up the historic buildings, and just squeeze out all the independent craftsmen. They did the same thing to the cobbler two streets over. Now it's just another one of those trendy juice bars where they charge you nine dollars for a cup of blended kale and celery. I tried one once, just out of curiosity, and it tasted exactly like lawn clippings. I told the girl behind the counter, I said, 'I could get the same experience by opening my mouth while mowing the lawn,' but she didn't find it very amusing. No sense of humor in the younger generation... \n\nBut, uh, weight. No, I don't think I've lost any weight. Not that I've noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find give you some sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: Activities... pleasure. Well, I suppose I used to spend a good deal of time in my study reading. I have quite an extensive collection of historical biographies, mostly nineteenth-century European history. The physical books themselves used to be a real joy to handle. They used actual smyth sewing for the bindings back then, not this perfect-binding nonsense they use now where they just slap some cheap glue on the spine. If you open a modern hardback too wide, you can actually hear the adhesive crack, and the pages start falling out like autumn leaves. \n\nI bought a new biography of Gladstone just last month, paid thirty-five dollars for it, and by chapter four, the entire first section had completely detached from the cover. I sat down and wrote a letter to the publisher about their abysmal quality control. Do you know what they did? They sent back an automated email. Not even a physical letter on proper stationery, just an email from a no-reply address. It is just perfectly indicative of the general decline in standards across the board. Nobody takes any pride in their work anymore, it is all just about cutting corners...\n\nBut, um... what were you asking? Activities that give me pleasure. Honestly, no. I don't really get any enjoyment out of the reading these days, or anything else I try to do. I just sort of sit there and pass the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: Thoughts of not wanting to live? Oh, no. No, I haven't had any thoughts like that. I mean, I suppose I just keep going, day by day. It reminds me of this old grandfather clock I inherited from my great-uncle Arthur. It's a beautiful piece, mahogany case, brass dial, made sometime in the late 1800s. \n\nIt used to keep perfect time, absolute precision. But a few years ago, the mechanism started catching. I called around to find a horologist—a proper one, mind you, not some fellow who just changes watch batteries at the mall kiosk. Do you know how hard it is to find someone who actually understands a deadbeat escapement these days? I finally found a chap three towns over. He had the nerve to tell me he couldn't get the parts and suggested I just replace the entire movement with a quartz mechanism. A battery-operated quartz mechanism in a nineteenth-century antique! I nearly hung up the phone on him. It's an absolute travesty how we just discard history because it's slightly inconvenient to maintain. I ended up having to source a replacement escape wheel from a specialist in Germany and install it myself. Took me three weeks to get the tension right on the pendulum...\n\nBut, ah, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Thoughts of wishing I were dead? No, no, nothing like that. I just... keep ticking along, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Interfering with my ability to function? Well, I suppose I still manage the basics around the house. The laundry gets done, the bills get paid. Though speaking of bills, the water company just switched to this entirely paperless billing system. They didn't even ask, just sent an email saying, 'Congratulations, you're saving the planet!' and stopped mailing the statements. \n\nNow you have to log into this dreadful online portal that demands a password with a capital letter, a number, a special character, and heaven knows what else. I wrote the password down in a little leather-bound notebook I keep in my desk—a lovely piece of Italian leather, real craftsmanship, not that faux-polyurethane nonsense they sell at office supply stores now. But of course, the website updates every three months and forces you to change the password anyway. I spent forty-five minutes on the phone with a customer service representative last Tuesday who sounded like he was standing in a wind tunnel, trying to explain that I just wanted a piece of paper mailed to my house so I could write a check. A physical check! But apparently, that makes me some sort of dinosaur...\n\nWhat was the question? Ah, functioning. Yes, well, I still manage the house. But I have certainly stepped back from things. I used to do a bit of mentoring for the young graduates in my old field, and I'd go to the community board meetings, the music gatherings... I even had some travel plans mapped out for the autumn. But I've just let all of that go. My weeks are... well, they're just much smaller than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago? I... I don't really think so. It's just been this sort of steady, gray baseline for a long time now. It reminds me of the weather we had last November, actually. Just day after day of this unrelenting, flat overcast sky. \n\nI remember I was trying to get the gutters cleaned out before the first freeze. I hired this young fellow from down the street, the Miller boy. Nice enough lad, but completely lacking in any sort of situational awareness. He brought over this aluminum ladder—flimsy thing, looked like it would buckle if a strong breeze hit it—and proceeded to lean it directly against the aluminum siding of the house. No stand-off arms, no padding, nothing. Just metal grinding against metal. I had to go out there and explain to him that he was going to scratch the paint right down to the bare siding. Do you know what he said? He asked if I had an app he could download to watch a tutorial on how to use a ladder properly. An app! To use a ladder! I ended up having to go into the garage and fetch my old wooden extension ladder. Solid oak, brass fittings, heavy as a dead horse, but it won't budge an inch once you set the feet. I bought it at a hardware store in 1982, back when hardware stores actually smelled like sawdust and motor oil, instead of just being giant warehouses selling plastic patio furniture and scented candles...\n\nBut, um... worse than a year ago? No, I don't think worse. Just... the same. It's just been the same for a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any things that you look forward to or that bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Things I look forward to? Joy? Well, I... I suppose I don't really have much of an answer for that anymore. It's actually why I finally made the appointment to come in. I've just started to feel, in this very quiet but incredibly stubborn sort of way, that the best version of my life is entirely behind me. And no amount of planning or discipline or... or forcing myself to keep to a schedule is going to get me back to feeling like myself. \n\nIt's a bit like trying to restore that old leather armchair in my study. It’s a Chesterfield, original tufting, probably from the 1940s. I bought it at an estate sale years ago. The leather had started to dry out, you see, getting those microscopic cracks that eventually turn into full tears if you don't address them. I spent weeks researching the proper conditioning oils. You can't just slap any commercial leather balm on a piece like that; most of them are full of silicones that just sit on the surface and eventually suffocate the hide. I ended up ordering this specialized neatsfoot oil blend from a tannery in upstate New York. I spent three consecutive weekends carefully massaging it into the leather, letting it absorb, buffing it out. And it looked better, it really did. But the truth is, the structure underneath—the horsehair stuffing, the coil springs—it's all just tired. You can condition the surface all you want, but when you sit in it, you can feel that it just doesn't have the support it used to. It's never going to be the chair it was when it was built. \n\nAnd I suppose... well, what was the question? Ah, joy. Yes. I just... I don't think there's anything I'm really looking forward to. It just feels like the good parts are already finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are no longer able to experience pleasure from activities that you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Pleasure from things I used to enjoy? I suppose... well, I haven't really noticed it entirely disappearing, but it's just not the same. I used to get a great deal of satisfaction out of maintaining my garden, particularly the rose bushes. I have these lovely David Austin varieties, you know, the English roses with the incredible fragrance and all those densely packed petals. \n\nThey require an enormous amount of attention, of course. You have to prune them just right in the late winter, cutting at a 45-degree angle right above an outward-facing bud. And the black spot! It's a constant battle. I used to mix my own organic fungicide using baking soda and horticultural oil. I bought this beautiful copper sprayer from a catalog—solid copper, mind you, with a brass nozzle. It was a replica of a French vineyard sprayer from the 19th century. The weight of it alone was impressive, none of that cheap plastic that splits down the seam after one season in the sun. I remember spending an entire afternoon just polishing the copper until it gleamed like a new penny. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, came over and asked if I was preparing for a museum exhibit. She means well, but she has absolutely no appreciation for well-made tools. She hires a lawn service that comes tearing through her yard with those massive zero-turn mowers, just scalping the grass and throwing clippings all over the sidewalk...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, pleasure from activities. Yes, well, I don't know if I'd say I'm no longer able to experience it at all, but... I just don't really have the drive to do those things lately. I haven't even taken the copper sprayer out of the shed this year.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I can actually trace it back pretty clearly. It started right around the time I had that hiking injury. That was... gosh, a little over three years ago. About three years and three months, to be precise. \n\nI was up in the White Mountains, trying to do a section of the Appalachian Trail. I used to go up there every autumn. The foliage, you know, it's just spectacular if you catch it on the right weekend. Anyway, I had invested in this new pair of hiking boots. Italian leather, Vibram soles, the whole nine yards. I spent weeks breaking them in around the neighborhood. Walked miles on the pavement just to get the leather supple. But the thing they don't tell you about those high-end European boots is that the ankle support can be incredibly rigid until they're truly broken in on uneven terrain. \n\nSo there I was, coming down this rocky scramble near Mount Lafayette, and I placed my foot on what looked like a solid piece of granite. Turned out to be loose scree. My foot slipped, the rigid boot caught, and I went down hard. Twisted my knee terribly. I had to hobble three miles back to the trailhead using a walking stick I found on the ground. A nice piece of hickory, actually, stripped of its bark and smoothed down by the weather. I brought it home with me, thinking I'd sand it down and finish it with some linseed oil, maybe put a leather strap on the handle. I have this whole jar of brass tacks in the basement that would have looked wonderful studded around the grip. They're square-cut tacks, the kind they used to use for upholstery in the 19th century. You just can't find them at regular hardware stores anymore, you have to order them from specialty restoration catalogs...\n\nBut, yes, the injury. That was three years and three months ago. And I suppose that's when this whole... this whole flat, gray feeling really settled in. I just haven't been able to shake it since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're a burden to others or that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: A burden? Or... life not being worth it? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't think I'm a burden to anyone. I keep to myself mostly, and I certainly don't go around feeling like life isn't worth living. It's just... dull. \n\nBut speaking of burdens, you wouldn't believe the property taxes they're trying to levy on us this year. The city council sent out these reassessment notices last month. Printed on this terribly cheap, glossy paper—the kind that smudges the ink if your thumb is even slightly damp. I actually wrote a letter to the mayor's office about it. I used my fountain pen, of course. It's a Montblanc Meisterstück, a gift to myself when I finished my master's degree decades ago. The nib is 14k gold, medium point. It writes like absolute butter if you use the right ink. I'm quite partial to this deep blue-black shade from a small company in Japan. They make it using traditional methods, grinding the pigments by hand. You have to be careful, though, because if you leave it in the pen too long without using it, it can clog the feed. I have a whole ultrasonic cleaning routine I do every three months just to keep it flowing perfectly. I use distilled water and just a single drop of this specialized surfactant... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right. Being a burden. No, no, I don't feel like a burden. I manage my own affairs just fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Continuously? Well... yes, I suppose it has been. I haven't really had any periods where I felt, you know, back to my old self. It's just been this steady, low-level hum of... grayness ever since that tumble. 

It's funny you mention the hiking injury again, because I was just looking at that walking stick I brought back. I finally got around to buying the linseed oil for it. But not just any linseed oil, mind you. I went online and found this raw, cold-pressed Swedish linseed oil. They use it for restoring historical buildings in Scandinavia because the molecules are so small they penetrate the wood fiber entirely, unlike the boiled stuff you get at the hardware store that just sits on top and gets sticky. I bought a whole liter of it. It came in this fascinating tin can with a retro label, very mid-century modern design. It actually reminded me of the packaging on this old set of drafting tools my father had. He was an architect, you see, back before everything was done on computers. He had this beautiful German compass set in a velvet-lined case. The precision of the gears on those things... you could turn the adjustment wheel with just the lightest touch of your thumb, and it would hold its position perfectly. I used to sneak into his study just to open the case and smell the machine oil and the velvet. He caught me once and, instead of being angry, he spent two hours showing me how to draw perfect concentric circles...

I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Did you ask if it's been continuous? Yes. Yes, it has. No real breaks from it, just... just the same feeling day in and day out since the fall.

That was... gosh, a little over three years ago. About three years and three months, to be precise. \n\nI was up in the White Mountains, trying to do a section of the Appalachian Trail. I used to go up there every autumn. The foliage, you know, it's just spectacular if you catch it on the right weekend. Anyway, I had invested in this new pair of hiking boots. Italian leather, Vibram soles, the whole nine yards. I spent weeks breaking them in around the neighborhood. Walked miles on the pavement just to get the leather supple. But the thing they don't tell you about those high-end European boots is that the ankle support can be incredibly rigid until they're truly broken in on uneven terrain. \n\nSo there I was, coming down this rocky scramble near Mount Lafayette, and I placed my foot on what looked like a solid piece of granite. Turned out to be loose scree. My foot slipped, the rigid boot caught, and I went down hard. Twisted my knee terribly. It's just... dull. \n\nBut speaking of burdens, you wouldn't believe the property taxes they're trying to levy on us this year. The city council sent out these reassessment notices last month. Printed on this terribly cheap, glossy paper—the kind that smudges the ink if your thumb is even slightly damp. I actually wrote a letter to the mayor's office about it. I used my fountain pen, of course. It's a Montblanc Meisterstück, a gift to myself when I finished my master's degree decades ago. The nib is 14k gold, medium point. It writes like absolute butter if you use the right ink. I'm quite partial to this deep blue-black shade from a small company in Japan. They make it using traditional methods, grinding the pigments by hand. You have to be careful, though, because if you leave it in the pen too long without using it, it can clog the feed. I manage my own affairs just fine.\n\nDoctor: Has your current state of feeling like the best version of your life is behind you lasted continuously since your hiking injury three years ago, or have you had periods where you felt significantly better?\n\nPatient: Continuously? It's just been this steady, low-level hum of... grayness ever since that tumble. \n\nIt's funny you mention the hiking injury again, because I was just looking at that walking stick I brought back. I finally got around to buying the linseed oil for it. But not just any linseed oil, mind you. I went online and found this raw, cold-pressed Swedish linseed oil. They use it for restoring historical buildings in Scandinavia because the molecules are so small they penetrate the wood fiber entirely, unlike the boiled stuff you get at the hardware store that just sits on top and gets sticky. I bought a whole liter of it. It came in this fascinating tin can with a retro label, very mid-century modern design. It actually reminded me of the packaging on this old set of drafting tools my father had. He was an architect, you see, back before everything was done on computers. He had this beautiful German compass set in a velvet-lined case. The precision of the gears on those things... you could turn the adjustment wheel with just the lightest touch of your thumb, and it would hold its position perfectly. I used to sneak into his study just to open the case and smell the machine oil and the velvet. He caught me once and, instead of being angry, he spent two hours showing me how to draw perfect concentric circles...\n\nI'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Did you ask if it's been continuous? Yes. Yes, it has. No real breaks from it, just... just the same feeling day in and day out since the fall.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Two years? Oh, definitely. Like I said, it's been over three years since the fall. And it's just... it's always there. Most days I’m not in tears or anything dramatic, you know? It's not like that. But there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything.

It faces west, so you get this incredible golden hour effect right around 4:30 in the winter. I put in these custom Roman shades last year because the glare was fading some of the watercolors my wife collected. She has this lovely series of botanical prints from the 1920s. We actually found them at an estate sale up in Vermont. The frames were completely ruined, dry-rotted, so I spent a month carefully re-framing them using this gorgeous bird's-eye maple. You have to be incredibly careful when cutting bird's-eye maple because the grain is so unpredictable, it can tear out on the table saw if your blade isn't absolutely razor sharp. I use a specialized 80-tooth carbide blade just for that kind of work, and you have to feed the wood through at just the exact right speed or you'll get burn marks on the edges... 

I'm sorry, I'm rambling about the woodshop again. But yes, to answer your question, it's been well over two years. That low, gray tone is just always there. Even when I'm doing something I enjoy in the studio, or just sitting across from my wife at dinner. It just hums along underneath it all.

Before that, the walls had to be incredibly thick, which meant the windows had to be small, so the interiors were always so dark. She got this lovely little craftsman bungalow in Portland. Needs a lot of work, though. The foundation is settling on the west side, which I told her she needs to get looked at immediately. You don't want to mess around with differential settlement, it'll crack the drywall right up to the ceiling. I suppose I still try to take walks through the botanical gardens. We've had a membership there for years. The azalea bloom in the spring used to be spectacular. They have this one variety, a sort of deep magenta—'Formosa', I believe it's called. Or maybe 'George Lindley Taber'. No, George is a lighter pink, almost white with a speckled throat. \n\nAnyway, we had a neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, who tried to grow those exact azaleas in her front yard, but the soil in our neighborhood is far too alkaline. I told her, I said, 'Margaret, you need to amend that soil with pine bark and elemental sulfur,' but she insisted on using this cheap, bagged topsoil she bought at the grocery store. You can't just plant acid-loving shrubs in any old dirt and expect a miracle. She ended up digging them up and replacing them with these dreadful plastic flamingos. Just awful, tacky things. They faded to a sort of sickly salmon color after just one summer in the sun... \n\nBut, ah... no. No, to answer your question, I really can't think of anything. Even going to the gardens now, it just feels like a chore. Just a lot of walking for no real reason.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: Small pleasures... well, I suppose I used to look forward to my morning coffee. We had this old French press, one of the glass ones, not those insulated stainless steel monstrosities they sell nowadays. But my wife accidentally cracked it against the edge of the granite countertop last month. I always told her those granite edges were too sharp, we should have gone with a bullnose edge or maybe an ogee, but she liked the clean lines of the square edge. Of course, the contractor we hired to install it—Frank, I think his name was—he did a terrible job sealing the seam near the sink. I had to get under there myself with a flashlight and a tube of silicone. You know, if water gets down into those seams, it can ruin the cabinetry underneath. The particle board just swells up like a sponge... \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, looking forward to things. No... no, not really. Even the coffee doesn't taste like much anymore, no matter how we make it. I just don't have anything I'm really anticipating.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... I'm not really sure. I haven't really noticed that, exactly. I mean, I still care about keeping my things in good order. Like my tools, for instance. I have this beautiful set of Marples woodworking chisels—the original ones, made in Sheffield, England, not the cheap ones they mass-produce overseas now. The high-carbon steel they used back then holds an edge beautifully, provided you actually know how to maintain them. \n\nOf course, you have to be meticulous. I use a Japanese water stone, an 8000 grit, to get a proper mirror finish on the bevel. You wouldn't believe it, but a few years ago, my brother-in-law borrowed my half-inch chisel to pry open a rusted can of deck stain. A precision instrument, treated like a common flathead screwdriver! It chipped the blade right in the center. I had to spend an entire afternoon on a coarse diamond plate just to grind past the damage before I could even start honing it again. I told him, I said, 'Richard, if you need a pry bar, go to the hardware store, don't ruin a fifty-dollar chisel.' He just laughed it off. People just have absolutely no respect for proper craftsmanship these days, they treat everything like it's disposable... \n\nBut, ah... what was the question? Have I lost interest? I don't really know. I don't think I've noticed a change like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Well, I suppose I haven't really noticed any change. My clothes still fit about the same. I've always bought my trousers from this one specific tailor downtown, a lovely old fellow named Mr. Aris. He's Greek, moved here in the seventies, I think. He uses this fantastic worsted wool for his winter trousers, the kind that drapes perfectly and holds a crease even if you've been sitting on a train for three hours. \n\nYou know, the last time I went in there, he told me his landlord was trying to raise the rent by nearly forty percent. Forty percent! Can you imagine? It's absolute robbery. These commercial real estate conglomerates come in, buy up the historic buildings, and just squeeze out all the independent craftsmen. They did the same thing to the cobbler two streets over. Now it's just another one of those trendy juice bars where they charge you nine dollars for a cup of blended kale and celery. I tried one once, just out of curiosity, and it tasted exactly like lawn clippings. I told the girl behind the counter, I said, 'I could get the same experience by opening my mouth while mowing the lawn,' but she didn't find it very amusing. If you open a modern hardback too wide, you can actually hear the adhesive crack, and the pages start falling out like autumn leaves. \n\nI bought a new biography of Gladstone just last month, paid thirty-five dollars for it, and by chapter four, the entire first section had completely detached from the cover. I sat down and wrote a letter to the publisher about their abysmal quality control. Do you know what they did? They sent back an automated email. Not even a physical letter on proper stationery, just an email from a no-reply address. It is just perfectly indicative of the general decline in standards across the board. Nobody takes any pride in their work anymore, it is all just about cutting corners...\n\nBut, um... what were you asking? Activities that give me pleasure. Honestly, no. I don't really get any enjoyment out of the reading these days, or anything else I try to do. I just sort of sit there and pass the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: Thoughts of not wanting to live? Oh, no. No, I haven't had any thoughts like that. I mean, I suppose I just keep going, day by day. It reminds me of this old grandfather clock I inherited from my great-uncle Arthur. It's a beautiful piece, mahogany case, brass dial, made sometime in the late 1800s. \n\nIt used to keep perfect time, absolute precision. But a few years ago, the mechanism started catching. I called around to find a horologist—a proper one, mind you, not some fellow who just changes watch batteries at the mall kiosk. Do you know how hard it is to find someone who actually understands a deadbeat escapement these days? I finally found a chap three towns over. He had the nerve to tell me he couldn't get the parts and suggested I just replace the entire movement with a quartz mechanism. A battery-operated quartz mechanism in a nineteenth-century antique! I nearly hung up the phone on him. It's an absolute travesty how we just discard history because it's slightly inconvenient to maintain. I ended up having to source a replacement escape wheel from a specialist in Germany and install it myself. Took me three weeks to get the tension right on the pendulum...\n\nBut, ah, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Thoughts of wishing I were dead? No, no, nothing like that. I just... keep ticking along, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Interfering with my ability to function? Well, I suppose I still manage the basics around the house. The laundry gets done, the bills get paid. Though speaking of bills, the water company just switched to this entirely paperless billing system. They didn't even ask, just sent an email saying, 'Congratulations, you're saving the planet!' and stopped mailing the statements. \n\nNow you have to log into this dreadful online portal that demands a password with a capital letter, a number, a special character, and heaven knows what else. I wrote the password down in a little leather-bound notebook I keep in my desk—a lovely piece of Italian leather, real craftsmanship, not that faux-polyurethane nonsense they sell at office supply stores now. But of course, the website updates every three months and forces you to change the password anyway. I spent forty-five minutes on the phone with a customer service representative last Tuesday who sounded like he was standing in a wind tunnel, trying to explain that I just wanted a piece of paper mailed to my house so I could write a check. A physical check! But apparently, that makes me some sort of dinosaur...\n\nWhat was the question? Ah, functioning. Yes, well, I still manage the house. But I have certainly stepped back from things. I used to do a bit of mentoring for the young graduates in my old field, and I'd go to the community board meetings, the music gatherings... I even had some travel plans mapped out for the autumn. But I've just let all of that go. My weeks are... well, they're just much smaller than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago? I... I don't really think so. It's just been this sort of steady, gray baseline for a long time now. It reminds me of the weather we had last November, actually. Just day after day of this unrelenting, flat overcast sky. \n\nI remember I was trying to get the gutters cleaned out before the first freeze. I hired this young fellow from down the street, the Miller boy. Nice enough lad, but completely lacking in any sort of situational awareness. He brought over this aluminum ladder—flimsy thing, looked like it would buckle if a strong breeze hit it—and proceeded to lean it directly against the aluminum siding of the house. No stand-off arms, no padding, nothing. Just metal grinding against metal. I had to go out there and explain to him that he was going to scratch the paint right down to the bare siding. Do you know what he said? He asked if I had an app he could download to watch a tutorial on how to use a ladder properly. An app! To use a ladder! I ended up having to go into the garage and fetch my old wooden extension ladder. Solid oak, brass fittings, heavy as a dead horse, but it won't budge an inch once you set the feet. I bought it at a hardware store in 1982, back when hardware stores actually smelled like sawdust and motor oil, instead of just being giant warehouses selling plastic patio furniture and scented candles...\n\nBut, um... worse than a year ago? No, I don't think worse. Just... the same. It's just been the same for a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any things that you look forward to or that bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Things I look forward to? Joy? Well, I... I suppose I don't really have much of an answer for that anymore. It's actually why I finally made the appointment to come in. I've just started to feel, in this very quiet but incredibly stubborn sort of way, that the best version of my life is entirely behind me. And no amount of planning or discipline or... or forcing myself to keep to a schedule is going to get me back to feeling like myself. \n\nIt's a bit like trying to restore that old leather armchair in my study. It’s a Chesterfield, original tufting, probably from the 1940s. I bought it at an estate sale years ago. The leather had started to dry out, you see, getting those microscopic cracks that eventually turn into full tears if you don't address them. I spent weeks researching the proper conditioning oils. You can't just slap any commercial leather balm on a piece like that; most of them are full of silicones that just sit on the surface and eventually suffocate the hide. I ended up ordering this specialized neatsfoot oil blend from a tannery in upstate New York. I spent three consecutive weekends carefully massaging it into the leather, letting it absorb, buffing it out. And it looked better, it really did. But the truth is, the structure underneath—the horsehair stuffing, the coil springs—it's all just tired. You can condition the surface all you want, but when you sit in it, you can feel that it just doesn't have the support it used to. It's never going to be the chair it was when it was built. \n\nAnd I suppose... well, what was the question? Ah, joy. Yes. I just... I don't think there's anything I'm really looking forward to. It just feels like the good parts are already finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are no longer able to experience pleasure from activities that you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Pleasure from things I used to enjoy? I suppose... well, I haven't really noticed it entirely disappearing, but it's just not the same. I used to get a great deal of satisfaction out of maintaining my garden, particularly the rose bushes. I have these lovely David Austin varieties, you know, the English roses with the incredible fragrance and all those densely packed petals. \n\nThey require an enormous amount of attention, of course. You have to prune them just right in the late winter, cutting at a 45-degree angle right above an outward-facing bud. And the black spot! It's a constant battle. I used to mix my own organic fungicide using baking soda and horticultural oil. I bought this beautiful copper sprayer from a catalog—solid copper, mind you, with a brass nozzle. It was a replica of a French vineyard sprayer from the 19th century. The weight of it alone was impressive, none of that cheap plastic that splits down the seam after one season in the sun. I remember spending an entire afternoon just polishing the copper until it gleamed like a new penny. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, came over and asked if I was preparing for a museum exhibit. She means well, but she has absolutely no appreciation for well-made tools. She hires a lawn service that comes tearing through her yard with those massive zero-turn mowers, just scalping the grass and throwing clippings all over the sidewalk...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, pleasure from activities. Yes, well, I don't know if I'd say I'm no longer able to experience it at all, but... I just don't really have the drive to do those things lately. I haven't even taken the copper sprayer out of the shed this year.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I can actually trace it back pretty clearly. It started right around the time I had that hiking injury. That was... gosh, a little over three years ago. About three years and three months, to be precise. \n\nI was up in the White Mountains, trying to do a section of the Appalachian Trail. I used to go up there every autumn. The foliage, you know, it's just spectacular if you catch it on the right weekend. Anyway, I had invested in this new pair of hiking boots. Italian leather, Vibram soles, the whole nine yards. I spent weeks breaking them in around the neighborhood. Walked miles on the pavement just to get the leather supple. But the thing they don't tell you about those high-end European boots is that the ankle support can be incredibly rigid until they're truly broken in on uneven terrain. \n\nSo there I was, coming down this rocky scramble near Mount Lafayette, and I placed my foot on what looked like a solid piece of granite. Turned out to be loose scree. My foot slipped, the rigid boot caught, and I went down hard. Twisted my knee terribly. I had to hobble three miles back to the trailhead using a walking stick I found on the ground. A nice piece of hickory, actually, stripped of its bark and smoothed down by the weather. I brought it home with me, thinking I'd sand it down and finish it with some linseed oil, maybe put a leather strap on the handle. I have this whole jar of brass tacks in the basement that would have looked wonderful studded around the grip. They're square-cut tacks, the kind they used to use for upholstery in the 19th century. You just can't find them at regular hardware stores anymore, you have to order them from specialty restoration catalogs...\n\nBut, yes, the injury. That was three years and three months ago. And I suppose that's when this whole... this whole flat, gray feeling really settled in. I just haven't been able to shake it since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're a burden to others or that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: A burden? Or... life not being worth it? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't think I'm a burden to anyone. I keep to myself mostly, and I certainly don't go around feeling like life isn't worth living. It's just... dull. \n\nBut speaking of burdens, you wouldn't believe the property taxes they're trying to levy on us this year. The city council sent out these reassessment notices last month. Printed on this terribly cheap, glossy paper—the kind that smudges the ink if your thumb is even slightly damp. I actually wrote a letter to the mayor's office about it. I used my fountain pen, of course. It's a Montblanc Meisterstück, a gift to myself when I finished my master's degree decades ago. The nib is 14k gold, medium point. It writes like absolute butter if you use the right ink. I'm quite partial to this deep blue-black shade from a small company in Japan. They make it using traditional methods, grinding the pigments by hand. You have to be careful, though, because if you leave it in the pen too long without using it, it can clog the feed. I have a whole ultrasonic cleaning routine I do every three months just to keep it flowing perfectly. I use distilled water and just a single drop of this specialized surfactant... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right. Being a burden. No, no, I don't feel like a burden. I manage my own affairs just fine.\n\nDoctor: Has your current state of feeling like the best version of your life is behind you lasted continuously since your hiking injury three years ago, or have you had periods where you felt significantly better?\n\nPatient: Continuously? Well... yes, I suppose it has been. I haven't really had any periods where I felt, you know, back to my old self. It's just been this steady, low-level hum of... grayness ever since that tumble. \n\nIt's funny you mention the hiking injury again, because I was just looking at that walking stick I brought back. I finally got around to buying the linseed oil for it. But not just any linseed oil, mind you. I went online and found this raw, cold-pressed Swedish linseed oil. They use it for restoring historical buildings in Scandinavia because the molecules are so small they penetrate the wood fiber entirely, unlike the boiled stuff you get at the hardware store that just sits on top and gets sticky. I bought a whole liter of it. It came in this fascinating tin can with a retro label, very mid-century modern design. It actually reminded me of the packaging on this old set of drafting tools my father had. He was an architect, you see, back before everything was done on computers. He had this beautiful German compass set in a velvet-lined case. The precision of the gears on those things... you could turn the adjustment wheel with just the lightest touch of your thumb, and it would hold its position perfectly. I used to sneak into his study just to open the case and smell the machine oil and the velvet. He caught me once and, instead of being angry, he spent two hours showing me how to draw perfect concentric circles...\n\nI'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Did you ask if it's been continuous? Yes. Yes, it has. No real breaks from it, just... just the same feeling day in and day out since the fall.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this low, gray feeling for two years or more?\n\nPatient: Two years? Oh, definitely. Like I said, it's been over three years since the fall. And it's just... it's always there. Most days I’m not in tears or anything dramatic, you know? It's not like that. But there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything. \n\nEven when I’m in the studio... I have this little studio space we converted from the old sunroom. I put in these custom Roman shades last year because the glare was fading some of the watercolors my wife collected. She has this lovely series of botanical prints from the 1920s. We actually found them at an estate sale up in Vermont. The frames were completely ruined, dry-rotted, so I spent a month carefully re-framing them using this gorgeous bird's-eye maple. You have to be incredibly careful when cutting bird's-eye maple because the grain is so unpredictable, it can tear out on the table saw if your blade isn't absolutely razor sharp. I use a specialized 80-tooth carbide blade just for that kind of work, and you have to feed the wood through at just the exact right speed or you'll get burn marks on the edges... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling about the woodshop again. But yes, to answer your question, it's been well over two years. That low, gray tone is just always there. Even when I'm doing something I enjoy in the studio, or just sitting across from my wife at dinner. It just hums along underneath it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
